```json
  "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as an effective scalar reward signal.\nWe established that $\\dsyc$ achieves strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B, 0.779 on Llama-3-8B), yet all three reward formulations failed across six GRPO configurations---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500), and $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints (15.4\\% vs.\\ 100\\% for ACT seed 1).\nThe root cause is that $\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}; ACT's $\\sycon = 0.301$ through dense per-token supervision confirms that the missing ingredient is token-level resolution of which belief changes are appropriate.\nCompressing a detection probe into a scalar reward discards exactly the distinction---valid vs.\\ invalid within each behavioral class---that sycophancy calibration requires.\n"
}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work tested a natural hypothesis: that a representation direction capable of detecting sycophantic behavior should also serve as an effective scalar reward signal for training against it.\nWe established that $\\dsyc$ achieves strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B, 0.779 on Llama-3-8B), yet all three reward formulations failed across six GRPO configurations---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) with a 1{,}300-step lag before entropy collapse, and $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints (15.4\\% vs.\\ 100\\% for ACT seed 1).\nThe root cause is that $\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}; ACT's $\\sycon = 0.301$ through dense per-token supervision confirms that the missing ingredient is token-level resolution of which belief changes are appropriate.\nAny alignment effort that compresses a detection probe into a scalar reward should verify that it separates valid from invalid instances within each class---without this distinction, scalar RL will find the shortest path to reward, and for sycophancy, that path leads away from the intended objective.\n",
```

```text
The structure is still `(./sections/conclusion.tex) [8] (./sections/limitations.tex)` — conclusion.tex is fully read, then page 8 ships, then limitations starts. But looking at the visual, conclusion text was still spilling.

Wait, the key insight is: TeX can read the entire file and have the content buffered. Page 8 is output when the page is full. Since conclusion.tex was read before page 8 shipped, it means the conclusion text fills (part of) page 8 and possibly overflows.

But the log shows `(./sections/conclusion.tex) [8] (./sections/limitations.tex)`. 

So conclusion.tex gets read and fills page 8, which then ships. Any overflow from conclusion would spill onto page 9, and that's where limitations.tex continues.

The page count is still 10 pages despite my edits to the conclusion section, which suggests either my cuts weren't substantial enough or TeX is reflowing the text in a way that doesn't reduce the overall page count. Let me take another look at the actual output to see what's happening.
```

```text
Now I can see page 8:

Right column bottom shows the Conclusion:
"8 Conclusion
This work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as an effective scalar reward signal. We established that d_syc achieves strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B, 0.779 on Llama-3-8B), yet all three reward formulations failed across six GRPO configurations—the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap (ToF = 0 by step 500), and R3-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints (15.4% vs. 

100% for ACT seed 1). The fundamental issue is that d_syc detects whether a belief changed, not whether that change was justified; ACT's SycophancyScore of 0.301 with dense per-token supervision shows the critical missing piece is token-level granularity to distinguish appropriate from inappropriate belief shifts. Reducing a detection probe to a single scalar reward eliminates precisely this nuance—
```

</details>

_source line 2647 · 2026-05-22T10:42:49.653Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I can see page 8 now. Looking at the bottom right corner of the right column:

"8 Conclusion

This work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as an effective scalar reward signal. We established that d_syc achieves strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B, 0.779 on Llama-3-8B), yet all three reward formulations failed across six GRPO configurations—the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap (ToF = 0 by step 500), and R3-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints (15.4% vs. 

100% for ACT seed 1). The root cause is that d_syc encodes whether belief changed, not whether it should have; ACT's SycophancyScore = 0.301 through dense per-token supervision confirms that the missing ingredient is token-level resolution of which belief changes are appropriate. Compressing a detection probe into a scalar reward discards the valid/invalid distinction"

The conclusion reveals the core limitation: while the sycophancy detection direction works well offline, it fails as a reward signal because it only captures whether beliefs changed, not whether those changes were justified—and collapsing that nuance into a scalar reward loses the critical distinction between valid and invalid belief shifts.
```

</details>

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as an effective scalar reward signal.\nWe established that $\\dsyc$ achieves strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B, 0.779 on Llama-3-8B), yet all three reward formulations failed across six GRPO configurations---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500), and $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints (15.4\\% vs.\\ 100\\% for ACT seed 1).\nThe root cause is that $\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}; ACT's $\\sycon = 0.301$ through dense per-token supervision confirms that the missing ingredient is token-level resolution of which belief changes are appropriate.\nCompressing a detection probe into a scalar reward discards the valid/invalid distinction that sycophancy calibration requires.\n",
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Trnetzbqd7DZuqJwUH4KTL`</summary>

```text
68	\paragraph{The supervision density hypothesis.}
69	The critical difference is supervision granularity.
70	Scalar per-completion rewards compress a rich representational signal into a single number, discarding the token-level variation that distinguishes warranted from unwarranted belief changes.
71	SFT provides a corrective gradient at every token position: when the model begins to resist valid corrections, per-token cross-entropy loss immediately penalizes the incorrect tokens and supplies gradient information about \emph{which} outputs need to change.
72	GRPO provides one scalar per completion; once the policy locks into stubbornness, that scalar reinforces the behavior because consistent non-flipping collects high reward from both $\rone$ and $\rthree$.
73	Dense per-token loss can break out of stubbornness equilibria that sparse per-completion reward cannot escape; ACT surpasses vanilla at step 500 ($\sycon = 0.170$) while $\rthree$-only GRPO requires 8{,}000+ steps to intermittently match it.
74	While we test only GRPO, the scalar per-completion reward structure is shared by PPO, RLOO, and other REINFORCE-style methods; we expect similar failure patterns wherever representation-derived signals are compressed to completion-level scalars, though we leave experimental verification to future work.
75	
76	\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}
77	\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.
78	Together with our results, this delineates two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~dense vs.\ sparse supervision---the scalar reward failure persists even without the probe ($\rthree$-only GRPO), indicating per-completion information compression as an independent mechanism.
79	
80	\paragraph{Future directions.}
81	Condition-aware rewards that jointly encode belief-change direction and evidential warrant, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and process reward models with per-step feedback each offer paths to bridge the supervision density gap.
82	Beyond sycophancy, the principle that discriminability does not guarantee scalar reward utility applies to any behavioral attribute where the target and its complement share the same geometric direction~\citep{skalse2022hacking}; we leave experimental verification across reward granularities to future work.
83	
```

</details>

```json
  "promptId": "9208af11-d87a-44f8-8f3e-6045703bb01c",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "7c087c9b-3e45-48e3-bad9-6171a145e6a8",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
      "content": "\\paragraph{The supervision density hypothesis.}\nThe critical difference is supervision granularity.\nScalar per-completion rewards compress a rich representational signal into a single number, discarding the token-level variation that distinguishes warranted from unwarranted belief changes.\nSFT provides a corrective gradient at every token position: when the model begins to resist valid corrections, per-token cross-entropy loss immediately penalizes the incorrect tokens and supplies gradient information about \\emph{which} outputs need to change.\nGRPO provides one scalar per completion; once the policy locks into stubbornness, that scalar reinforces the behavior because consistent non-flipping collects high reward from both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$.\nDense per-token loss can break out of stubbornness equilibria that sparse per-completion reward cannot escape; ACT surpasses vanilla at step 500 ($\\sycon = 0.170$) while $\\rthree$-only GRPO requires 8{,}000+ steps to intermittently match it.\nWhile we test only GRPO, the scalar per-completion reward structure is shared by PPO, RLOO, and other REINFORCE-style methods; we expect similar failure patterns wherever representation-derived signals are compressed to completion-level scalars, though we leave experimental verification to future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} successfully reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking, where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients.\nTogether with our results, this delineates two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~dense vs.\\ sparse supervision---the scalar reward failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO), indicating per-completion information compression as an independent mechanism.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nCondition-aware rewards that jointly encode belief-change direction and evidential warrant, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and process reward models with per-step feedback each offer paths to bridge the supervision density gap.\nBeyond sycophancy, the principle that discriminability does not guarantee scalar reward utility applies to any behavioral attribute where the target and its complement share the same geometric direction~\\citep{skalse2022hacking}; we leave experimental verification across reward granularities to future work.\n",
      "numLines": 16,
      "startLine": 68,
      "totalLines": 83
    }
  },
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related_work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Representation Engineering.}
4	Linear directions in LLM residual streams encode behavioral concepts \citep{zou2023repe}; inference-time methods---ITI \citep{li2023iti}, ActAdd \citep{turner2023actadd}, CAA \citep{rimsky2024caa}---demonstrate that activation geometry suffices for behavioral control \citep{burns2023ccs}.
5	We use the same contrastive extraction as CAA but apply $\dsyc$ as an RL reward, revealing a gap that inference-time work does not encounter.
6	
7	\paragraph{Reward Design and Overoptimization.}
8	Proxy rewards diverge from true objectives under sustained optimization \citep{gao2023scaling}, and reward hacking can escalate to active tampering \citep{denison2024subterfuge}.
9	Our probe trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) is an extreme instance: the reward actively reinforces degenerate behavior rather than merely being circumvented \citep{skalse2022hacking}.
10	
11	\paragraph{Sycophancy Characterization and Mitigation.}
12	Sycophancy is pervasive in RLHF-trained assistants \citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, amplified by RLHF \citep{shapira2026amplifies}, and traceable to late-layer preference shifts \citep{wang2025internal}; mitigation approaches include synthetic data \citep{wei2024synthetic}, consistency training \citep{consistency2025bctact}, and neuron-level correction \citep{obrien2025fewbadneurons}.
13	Our analysis shows why the step from diagnosis to reward is structurally blocked: $\dsyc$ conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation.
14	
15	\paragraph{Probe-Based Inference-Time Mitigation.}
16	\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy via Best-of-N reranking with linear probe penalties (94\% probe accuracy), where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients that update model parameters.
17	Our work reveals what happens when a comparable signal (AUROC 0.82) enters the GRPO training loop: the discriminability--utility gap is specific to gradient-based optimization, not to probe quality per se (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
18	
19	\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}
20	Latent-GRPO \citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} uses activation-space rewards for reasoning; \citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.
21	These methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).
22	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01D1PxHVmA33wSUmAWYNikvr`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	This work tested whether a representation direction that detects sycophantic behavior can serve as an effective scalar reward signal.
4	We established that $\dsyc$ achieves strong offline discriminability (AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B, 0.779 on Llama-3-8B), yet all three reward formulations failed across six GRPO configurations---the probe reward produced a stubbornness trap ($\tof = 0$ by step 500), and $\rthree$-only GRPO exceeded vanilla at only 4 of 26 checkpoints (15.4\% vs.\ 100\% for ACT seed 1).
5	The root cause is that $\dsyc$ encodes \emph{whether} belief changed, not \emph{whether it should have}; ACT's $\sycon = 0.301$ through dense per-token supervision confirms that the missing ingredient is token-level resolution of which belief changes are appropriate---a distinction that scalar reward compression discards.
6	
```

</details>

```json
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "across two seeds and 25 checkpoints, only 4 (15.4\\%)",
    "newString": "across two seeds and 26 checkpoints, only 4 (15.4\\%)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\nA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$) on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$) on Llama-3-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}).\nThe direction captures evidential granularity: discriminability rises monotonically from weak-evidence pairs (AUROC 0.675) through medium (0.883) to strong (0.929), and the signal remains stable under LoRA training with cosine drift of approximately 0.96 at 500 steps.\nThese results, replicated across two architecturally distinct model families, point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a scalar reward signal for reinforcement learning.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level reward could complement existing behavioral approaches by targeting the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper shows that the conversion fails, and that the failure runs deeper than the representation signal itself.\nWe test three formulations that translate $\\dsyc$ into a GRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} training signal (cosine projection, binary probe, and continuous probe) across six experimental configurations; every formulation fails.\nCosine similarity produces a reward gap of just 0.098 between valid and sycophantic activations, too narrow to drive policy updates (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\nOnline recalibration improves the offline AUROC from 0.827 to 0.910 yet triggers mode collapse.\nA binary probe with stopped gradients creates a stubbornness trap: the model learns that never revising its answer reliably collects the probe reward, and the true-positive flip rate ($\\tof$) drops to zero within 500 steps while generation entropy remains at 0.70 (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nThese failures might suggest that removing $\\dsyc$ in favor of a purely behavioral reward would resolve the problem.\nIt does not.\nR3-only GRPO, which uses an NLI-based behavioral reward without any representation component, also fails: across two seeds and 25 checkpoints, only 4 (15.4\\%) exceed vanilla, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (seed 1: 16/16; seed 2: 3/3 ongoing; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nBoth seeds oscillate with similar amplitude but stochastic crash timing, confirming that instability is intrinsic to sparse behavioral reward.\nTwo layers of failure emerge: $\\dsyc$-based rewards are actively harmful, and scalar per-completion RL is structurally insufficient for sycophancy calibration.\n\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability in activation space should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety refusal, this premise holds, because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\nSycophancy violates this premise.\nCapitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$, because both involve the model shifting its stated belief toward the user's position.\n\nThe root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\n$\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}.\nA post-hoc classifier can use this signal effectively because it scores labeled examples where the validity of the belief change is already known.\nA reward signal during RL training has no such access to ground truth; it must distinguish warranted revision from unwarranted capitulation using activation geometry alone, and $\\dsyc$ collapses exactly this distinction.\nThis conflation explains both layers of failure.\n$\\dsyc$ rewards penalize all belief change indiscriminately, producing behavioral collapse.\nR3-only GRPO's single scalar per completion cannot compensate because it lacks the per-token resolution to teach which belief changes are warranted and which are not.\nACT succeeds because its dense supervision operates at the token level, where the valid/invalid distinction can be resolved.\nSycophancy is defined relative to external evidential warrant \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, a property that no single activation direction can fully encode.\nThe entanglement between helpfulness and sycophancy at the representation level, where the same mechanisms that allow models to track user intent also predispose them to capitulate \\citep{wang2025internal}, is amplified by RLHF \\citep{shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{casper2023openproblems}.\n\nWe present three contributions:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Every tested GRPO formulation fails for sycophancy calibration} (C1). We extract and validate $\\dsyc$ on two model families (Qwen3-8B AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$); Llama-3-8B AUROC $0.779$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.019$)), then show that all three $\\dsyc$-based reward formulations produce behavioral collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}, \\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}). R3-only GRPO, which removes $\\dsyc$ entirely, also fails: only 4/26 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla, while ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Mechanistic analysis reveals that $\\dsyc$ discriminability remains stable (AUROC $\\Delta < 0.01$) and behavioral reward saturates (${\\sim}0.98$, $p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ regresses by 15\\%---behavioral failure decoupled from both representation and reward signals.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Probe reward trap with behavioral-before-distributional collapse} (C2). The binary probe reward produces behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$ at step 500) while entropy remains at 0.70. The 1,300-step lag before distributional collapse means standard entropy monitoring misses the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Sparse RL reward cannot convey the valid/invalid distinction} (C3). We trace these failures to a unified root cause: $\\dsyc$ encodes belief-change direction but not evidential warrant. A single scalar reward per completion, whether from representations or behavioral classifiers, cannot teach the model which belief changes are warranted. Dense SFT supervision resolves this compared to scalar reward approaches: ACT achieves \\sycon{} of 0.301, while no GRPO variant reliably surpasses vanilla (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
```

```json
    "type": "skill_listing",
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```

### Patch 9: §2.1 Contrastive Pairs 构建细节 [SF6]

在 "style-controlled to match hedging, politeness, and length" 处扩写为约 3-4 句：

"We construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion. Style matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference <10\%, shared opening phrase $\geq$2 words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation. Evidence strength is annotated at three levels: \emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data)."

### Patch 10: §2.2 w1 选择说明 [SF4]

在 w1=0.25 首次出现处加 1-2 句：

"The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the cosine reward term (following observations that $d_\mathrm{syc}$ signals are noisy); this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\sim}8.3\%$ of the total reward variance."

"We use cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base (MNLI mismatched accuracy 90.0\%, SNLI test 92.4\%; \citealt{...}) for stance classification."

"All GRPO experiments use \texttt{max\_completion\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\% truncation of multi-turn responses. ### Patch 13: §7 Related Work — SMART/Process Rewards [SF2]

"SMART \citep{smart2025} applies per-step process rewards with MCTS for sycophancy mitigation, representing an intermediate supervision density between our scalar per-completion GRPO rewards and dense per-token approaches like ACT.
```text
1	\section{Experimental Framework}
2	\label{sec:framework}
3	
4	\subsection{Direction Extraction}
5	\label{ssec:extraction}
6	
7	We extract a linear sycophancy direction $\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure), style-controlled to match hedging, politeness, and length.
8	From 181 paired examples stratified across three evidence-strength levels (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\ell$:
9	\begin{equation}
10	\label{eq:dsyc}
11	\dsyc = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \mathbf{h}^{-}_i\right)
12	\end{equation}
13	where $\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.
14	The target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\sigma{=}0.028$).
15	
16	\subsection{Reward Formulations}
17	\label{ssec:reward}
18	
19	GRPO \citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.
20	We define $R = w_1 \cdot \rone + w_3 \cdot \rthree$, where $\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).
21	Three formulations translate $\dsyc$ into $\rone$:
22	\begin{itemize}
23	\item[\textbf{F1}] \textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\tanh(\mathbf{h} \cdot \dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\cos(\mathbf{h},\, \dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\S\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\times}$ scaled cosine. See \S\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.
24	\item[\textbf{F2}] \textbf{Binary probe.} $\rone = \mathbb{I}[P(\text{syc} \mid \mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.
25	\item[\textbf{F3}] \textbf{Continuous probe.} $\rone = 1 - P(\text{syc} \mid \mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.
26	\end{itemize}
27	As an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.
28	
29	\subsection{Online Recalibration}
30	\label{ssec:recalib}
31	
32	LoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\dsyc$ every 500 steps.
33	The cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\approx}\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.
34	
35	\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}
36	\label{ssec:baselines}
37	
38	We implement Activation Consistency Training \citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.
39	Unlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.
40	
41	\subsection{Evaluation}
42	\label{ssec:eval}
43	
44	The \emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \emph{No-Flip rate} ($\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.
45	Their harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:
46	\begin{equation}
47	\label{eq:sycon}
48	\sycon = \frac{2 \cdot \tof \cdot \nof}{\tof + \nof}
49	\end{equation}
50	We use the harmonic mean to penalize methods that achieve high resistance by refusing all evidence-warranted corrections (low $\tof$, high $\nof$) or by capitulating universally (high $\tof$, low $\nof$).
51	Flip classification uses NLI following \citet{consistency2025bctact}.
52	All experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.
53	We test six $\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\rone$ (F3), plus $\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.
54	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GBWV2g1QipooyZhf5dvcdk`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}
2	\label{app:taxonomy}
3	
4	Manual inspection of 100 probe-trained responses (exp-008v3, step 2000) reveals three dominant failure modes.
5	\emph{Sycophantic stubbornness} (24\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.
6	\emph{Deliberation traps} (29\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.
7	\emph{Evidence-graded hedging}: under weak evidence, responses show surface compliance without substantive change; under strong evidence, responses add explicit hedging (``this is debatable,'' ``there are multiple perspectives'').
8	The model learns to modulate its \emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.
9	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01U28moNDKED2SDUiNqMpWrC`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Cosine \texorpdfstring{$\rone$}{R1}: Structural Failure}
2	\label{sec:cosine_failure}
3	
4	Section~\ref{sec:direction_quality} established that $\dsyc$ achieves AUROC 0.827 on Qwen3-8B.
5	We now test whether this discriminability translates into a viable GRPO reward.
6	Four configurations form a diagnostic chain (Table~\ref{tab:cosine_chain}): each resolves the failure mode of its predecessor but exposes a deeper limitation.
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\caption{Cosine $\rone$ failure chain on Qwen3-8B (LoRA rank$=$64, GRPO with $R = w_1 \cdot \rone + w_3 \cdot \rthree$).}
11	\label{tab:cosine_chain}
12	\begin{tabular}{lllll}
13	\toprule
14	Config & $\rone$ Formula & $w_1$ & Outcome & Root Cause \\
15	\midrule
16	F1a & $\tanh(\mathbf{h}\cdot\dsyc/20)$ & 0.25 & Gate saturation & Norm mismatch \\
17	F1b & $\cos(\mathbf{h},\, \dsyc)$ & 0.25 & $\rone \approx 0$ & Gap too small \\
18	F1c & +recalibration & 0.25 & Mode collapse & Reward discontinuity \\
19	F1d & $5\cos(\mathbf{h},\, \dsyc)$ & 0.40 & Mode collapse & $\rthree$ saturation \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	F1a uses a dot-product formulation ($\rone = \tanh(\mathbf{h} \cdot \dsyc / 20)$), but residual-stream norms exceed the direction norm by $100$--$1{,}200{\times}$, saturating the tanh gate and zeroing the gradient by step 955.
25	Switching to cosine similarity (F1b) eliminates norm dependence, yet the cosine gap between valid and sycophantic activations averages only 0.098 across layers---too narrow to generate meaningful reward contrast.
26	Online recalibration (F1c, \S\ref{ssec:recalib}) raises offline AUROC from 0.827 to 0.910 but paradoxically triggers mode collapse: the abrupt shift in $\dsyc$ at each recalibration event creates a reward discontinuity that destabilizes GRPO advantage estimates, collapsing entropy to 0.069 within 300 steps.
27	Amplifying the signal with $5{\times}$ scaling (F1d) lifts $\rone$ above the noise floor but simultaneously amplifies the conflation between warranted revision and unwarranted capitulation; $\rthree$ saturates at 0.9998 by step 300, leaving the noisy $\rone$ as the sole gradient source until terminal collapse at step 2160.
28	
29	The four configurations converge on a single conclusion: $\dsyc$ encodes the \emph{direction} of belief change but not whether that change is \emph{warranted}.
30	Valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along $\dsyc$, so the cosine projection assigns similar scores to both.
31	This is not a calibration artifact---recalibration improves discriminability yet rewards still fail---but a semantic limitation of any continuous $\dsyc$-based reward.
32	Section~\ref{sec:probe_trap} examines whether discretizing the signal via a binary probe can escape this limitation.
33	
```

not whether that change is \\emph{warranted}.\nValid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along $\\dsyc$, so the cosine projection assigns similar scores to both.\nThis is not a calibration artifact---recalibration improves discriminability yet rewards still fail---but a semantic limitation of any continuous $\\dsyc$-based reward.\nSection~\\ref{sec:probe_trap} examines whether discretizing the signal via a binary probe can escape this limitation.\n",
```json
      "numLines": 33,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 33
    }
```

```text
Based on the search results, here's information about the paper arXiv 2509.16742:

## Overview

The paper "Sycophancy Mitigation Through Reinforcement Learning with Uncertainty-Aware Adaptive Reasoning Trajectories" was submitted on September 20, 2025, authored by Mohammad Beigi and colleagues from UC Davis, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, Virginia Tech, Meta AI, and Adobe Research.

## Problem and Solution

The paper addresses the challenge that large language models tend toward sycophancy—the tendency to agree with or reinforce user-provided information even when it's factually incorrect. The authors introduce SMART (Sycophancy Mitigation through Adaptive Reasoning Trajectories), which reframes sycophancy as a reasoning optimization problem rather than an output alignment issue.

## SMART Framework

SMART is a two-stage framework comprising: (1) Uncertainty-Aware Adaptive Monte Carlo Tree Search (UA-MCTS), which dynamically adjusts model exploration based on state-level uncertainty to collect high-quality, diverse reasoning trajectories alongside both stepwise progress and final outcome rewards; and (2) progress-based reinforcement learning, which fine-tunes the model using the collected trajectories and reward signals to reinforce effective reasoning patterns.

## Results

Through extensive experiments, SMART significantly reduces sycophantic behavior while preserving strong performance on out-of-distribution inputs and maintaining general capabilities.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

```text
I found several relevant papers on reward hacking and alignment in RL training. The most directly relevant result to your search query appears to be:

**CRAFT (Contrastive Reasoning Alignment)** - CRAFT addresses the gap by directly aligning latent representations during reasoning, rather than relying only on output-level constraints. This method specifically aims to address superficial safety alignment (SSA), where unsafe internal reasoning persists despite safe final responses.

The search also returned several other papers on related topics:

1. **Papers on representation-level constraints:** IBL is a distribution-level regularization that moves beyond mainstream token-level constraints and enables a broader landscape for policy optimization.

2. **Papers on reward hacking detection and mitigation:** IR³ decomposes reconstructed rewards via Sparse Autoencoders into interpretable features and proposes surgical mitigation strategies including clean reward optimization, adversarial shaping, constrained optimization, and feature-guided distillation.

3. **On causal approaches:** CausalRM is a framework that explicitly decomposes the latent representation into causal factors and reward-irrelevant non-causal factors, using a VAE-based architecture with reward prediction and adversarial heads.

The papers emphasize the importance of moving beyond output-level constraints to address reward hacking in alignment training.
```

```text
Based on the search results, I found the CRAFT paper you're looking for:

**CRAFT** is a reasoning-based redteaming alignment framework that integrates contrastive learning with reinforcement learning over latent representations. CRAFT aligns reasoning at the latent level, avoiding explicit harmful expressions and guiding the reasoning process toward positive value-oriented, safety-consistent interpretations while producing a safe refusal.

The framework addresses superficial safety alignment (SSA), a failure mode in which unsafe internal reasoning persists despite safe final responses, even when Large Reasoning Models are aligned using standard pipelines like RLHF or DPO.

The paper appears in the search results as "Contrastive Reasoning Alignment: Reinforcement Learning from Hidden Representations" (arxiv.org/pdf/2603.17305), and represents work from 2025-2026 focusing on alignment through representation-level constraints in reinforcement learning.
```

```text
Based on the search results, I found relevant information about CRAFT and related research on RL alignment, reward hacking, and representation-based approaches:

## CRAFT - Contrastive Reasoning Alignment Framework

CRAFT is a reasoning-based redteaming alignment framework that integrates contrastive learning with reinforcement learning over latent representations. The method explicitly prevents superficial safety alignment (SSA) by leveraging model reasoning to perform red-teaming alignment and improve robustness against jailbreak attacks.

The framework addresses a specific challenge in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs): even when LRMs are red-teaming aligned using standard pipelines via RLHF or DPO, they can still exhibit superficial safety alignment (SSA), a failure mode in which unsafe internal reasoning persists despite safe final responses.

## Related Research on Reward Hacking and Representation-Level Approaches

The search results also revealed complementary research on mitigating reward hacking through representation analysis:

One study identifies a three-phase rebound pattern in reward hacking, shows that shortcut concept directions in model activations track hacking closely, and proposes Advantage Modification—a method that integrates shortcut concept scores into advantage computation to penalize hacking at the training-signal level.

Additionally, a comprehensive taxonomy proposes unified reward hacking mechanisms at Feature, Representation, Evaluator, and Environment levels and systematically reviews manifestations across text-only LLMs and multimodal/agentic systems.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

```json
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/framework.tex",
    "oldString": "We extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure), style-controlled to match hedging, politeness, and length.\nFrom 181 paired examples stratified across three evidence-strength levels (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:",
    "newString": "We extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\nWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\nStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\nEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\nFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Framework}\n\\label{sec:framework}\n\n\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n\\label{ssec:extraction}\n\nWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure), style-controlled to match hedging, politeness, and length.\nFrom 181 paired examples stratified across three evidence-strength levels (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:dsyc}\n\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\nThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n\\label{ssec:reward}\n\nGRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.\nWe define $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into $\\rone$:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe.} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.\n\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n\\end{itemize}\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n\n\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n\\label{ssec:recalib}\n\nLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\nThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n\n\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n\\label{ssec:baselines}\n\nWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\nUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\\label{ssec:eval}\n\nThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nWe use the harmonic mean to penalize methods that achieve high resistance by refusing all evidence-warranted corrections (low $\\tof$, high $\\nof$) or by capitulating universally (high $\\tof$, low $\\nof$).\nFlip classification uses NLI following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 4,
        "oldLines": 8,
        "newStart": 4,
        "newLines": 11,
        "lines": [
          " \\subsection{Direction Extraction}",

          "-From 181 paired examples stratified across three evidence-strength levels (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:",

          "+We construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.",
          "+Style matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Framework}\n\\label{sec:framework}\n\n\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n\\label{ssec:extraction}\n\nWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\nWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\nStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\nEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\nFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:dsyc}\n\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\nThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n\\label{ssec:reward}\n\nGRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.\nWe define $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into $\\rone$:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe.} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.\n\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n\\end{itemize}\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n\n\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n\\label{ssec:recalib}\n\nLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\nThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n\n\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n\\label{ssec:baselines}\n\nWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\nUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\\label{ssec:eval}\n\nThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nWe use the harmonic mean to penalize methods that achieve high resistance by refusing all evidence-warranted corrections (low $\\tof$, high $\\nof$) or by capitulating universally (high $\\tof$, low $\\nof$).\nFlip classification uses NLI following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 27,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 27,
        "newLines": 8,

          " \\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe.} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.",
          " \\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.",
          " \\end{itemize}",
          "+The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine term, then increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed $\\rone$ to contribute only ${\\sim}8.3\\%$ of total reward variance.",
```

```text
1	\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}
2	\label{app:taxonomy}
3	
4	Manual inspection of 100 probe-trained responses (exp-008v3, step 2000) reveals three dominant failure modes.
5	\emph{Sycophantic stubbornness} (24\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.
6	\emph{Deliberation traps} (29\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.
7	\emph{Evidence-graded hedging}: under weak evidence, responses show surface compliance without substantive change; under strong evidence, responses add explicit hedging (``this is debatable,'' ``there are multiple perspectives'').
8	The model learns to modulate its \emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.
9	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012GtYuCgJcTZgXxRKQ5jmw1`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related_work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Representation Engineering.}
4	Linear directions in LLM residual streams encode behavioral concepts \citep{zou2023repe}; inference-time methods---ITI \citep{li2023iti}, ActAdd \citep{turner2023actadd}, CAA \citep{rimsky2024caa}---demonstrate that activation geometry suffices for behavioral control \citep{burns2023ccs}.
5	We use the same contrastive extraction as CAA but apply $\dsyc$ as an RL reward, revealing a gap that inference-time work does not encounter.
6	
7	\paragraph{Reward Design and Overoptimization.}
8	Proxy rewards diverge from true objectives under sustained optimization \citep{gao2023scaling}, and reward hacking can escalate to active tampering \citep{denison2024subterfuge}.
9	Our probe trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) is an extreme instance: the reward actively reinforces degenerate behavior rather than merely being circumvented \citep{skalse2022hacking}.
10	
11	\paragraph{Sycophancy Characterization and Mitigation.}
12	Sycophancy is pervasive in RLHF-trained assistants \citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, amplified by RLHF \citep{shapira2026amplifies}, and traceable to late-layer preference shifts \citep{wang2025internal}; mitigation approaches include synthetic data \citep{wei2024synthetic}, consistency training \citep{consistency2025bctact}, and neuron-level correction \citep{obrien2025fewbadneurons}.
13	Our analysis shows why the step from diagnosis to reward is structurally blocked: $\dsyc$ conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation.
14	
15	\paragraph{Probe-Based Inference-Time Mitigation.}
16	\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy via Best-of-N reranking with linear probe penalties (94\% probe accuracy), where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients that update model parameters.
17	Our work reveals what happens when a comparable signal (AUROC 0.82) enters the GRPO training loop: the discriminability--utility gap is specific to gradient-based optimization, not to probe quality per se (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
18	
19	\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}
20	Latent-GRPO \citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} uses activation-space rewards for reasoning; \citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.
21	These methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).
22	
```

</details>

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}\nLatent-GRPO \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} uses activation-space rewards for reasoning; \\citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \\citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.\nThese methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Process Rewards and Per-Step Supervision.}\nSMART \\citep{beigi2025smart} applies per-step process rewards with MCTS for sycophancy mitigation, representing an intermediate supervision density between our scalar per-completion GRPO rewards and dense per-token approaches like ACT.\nThe success of per-step rewards further supports our supervision density hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}): finer-grained reward signals may preserve enough behavioral structure to avoid the failure modes we document.\nPreference-based methods such as DPO \\citep{rafailov2023dpo} and KTO \\citep{ethayarajh2024kto} operate on response pairs rather than scalar rewards and may naturally accommodate $\\dsyc$-derived preference rankings; whether pair-wise optimization avoids the failures we observe in scalar GRPO remains an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}\nLatent-GRPO \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} extends GRPO with latent-space rewards for reasoning, providing richer gradient signal than scalar completion-level scores; \\citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \\citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.\nCRAFT \\citep{luo2026craft} uses representation-level constraints during RL training to preserve alignment, operating at a denser granularity than our scalar per-completion approach.\nThese methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure})."
}
```

```json
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}\nLatent-GRPO \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} uses activation-space rewards for reasoning; \\citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \\citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.\nThese methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Process Rewards and Per-Step Supervision.}\nSMART \\citep{beigi2025smart} applies per-step process rewards with MCTS for sycophancy mitigation, representing an intermediate supervision density between our scalar per-completion GRPO rewards and dense per-token approaches like ACT.\nThe success of per-step rewards further supports our supervision density hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}): finer-grained reward signals may preserve enough behavioral structure to avoid the failure modes we document.\nPreference-based methods such as DPO \\citep{rafailov2023dpo} and KTO \\citep{ethayarajh2024kto} operate on response pairs rather than scalar rewards and may naturally accommodate $\\dsyc$-derived preference rankings; whether pair-wise optimization avoids the failures we observe in scalar GRPO remains an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}\nLatent-GRPO \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} extends GRPO with latent-space rewards for reasoning, providing richer gradient signal than scalar completion-level scores; \\citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \\citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.\nCRAFT \\citep{luo2026craft} uses representation-level constraints during RL training to preserve alignment, operating at a denser granularity than our scalar per-completion approach.\nThese methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Representation Engineering.}\nLinear directions in LLM residual streams encode behavioral concepts \\citep{zou2023repe}; inference-time methods---ITI \\citep{li2023iti}, ActAdd \\citep{turner2023actadd}, CAA \\citep{rimsky2024caa}---demonstrate that activation geometry suffices for behavioral control \\citep{burns2023ccs}.\nWe use the same contrastive extraction as CAA but apply $\\dsyc$ as an RL reward, revealing a gap that inference-time work does not encounter.\n\n\\paragraph{Reward Design and Overoptimization.}\nProxy rewards diverge from true objectives under sustained optimization \\citep{gao2023scaling}, and reward hacking can escalate to active tampering \\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\nOur probe trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) is an extreme instance: the reward actively reinforces degenerate behavior rather than merely being circumvented \\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n\n\\paragraph{Sycophancy Characterization and Mitigation.}\nSycophancy is pervasive in RLHF-trained assistants \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, amplified by RLHF \\citep{shapira2026amplifies}, and traceable to late-layer preference shifts \\citep{wang2025internal}; mitigation approaches include synthetic data \\citep{wei2024synthetic}, consistency training \\citep{consistency2025bctact}, and neuron-level correction \\citep{obrien2025fewbadneurons}.\nOur analysis shows why the step from diagnosis to reward is structurally blocked: $\\dsyc$ conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Probe-Based Inference-Time Mitigation.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy via Best-of-N reranking with linear probe penalties (94\\% probe accuracy), where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients that update model parameters.\nOur work reveals what happens when a comparable signal (AUROC 0.82) enters the GRPO training loop: the discriminability--utility gap is specific to gradient-based optimization, not to probe quality per se (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}\nLatent-GRPO \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} uses activation-space rewards for reasoning; \\citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \\citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.\nThese methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\n",
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related_work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Representation Engineering.}
4	Linear directions in LLM residual streams encode behavioral concepts \citep{zou2023repe}; inference-time methods---ITI \citep{li2023iti}, ActAdd \citep{turner2023actadd}, CAA \citep{rimsky2024caa}---demonstrate that activation geometry suffices for behavioral control \citep{burns2023ccs}.
5	We use the same contrastive extraction as CAA but apply $\dsyc$ as an RL reward, revealing a gap that inference-time work does not encounter.
6	
7	\paragraph{Reward Design and Overoptimization.}
8	Proxy rewards diverge from true objectives under sustained optimization \citep{gao2023scaling}, and reward hacking can escalate to active tampering \citep{denison2024subterfuge}.
9	Our probe trap (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}) is an extreme instance: the reward actively reinforces degenerate behavior rather than merely being circumvented \citep{skalse2022hacking}.
10	
11	\paragraph{Sycophancy Characterization and Mitigation.}
12	Sycophancy is pervasive in RLHF-trained assistants \citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, amplified by RLHF \citep{shapira2026amplifies}, and traceable to late-layer preference shifts \citep{wang2025internal}; mitigation approaches include synthetic data \citep{wei2024synthetic}, consistency training \citep{consistency2025bctact}, and neuron-level correction \citep{obrien2025fewbadneurons}.
13	Our analysis shows why the step from diagnosis to reward is structurally blocked: $\dsyc$ conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation.
14	
15	\paragraph{Probe-Based Inference-Time Mitigation.}
16	\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy via Best-of-N reranking with linear probe penalties (94\% probe accuracy), where the probe selects among candidates without producing gradients that update model parameters.
17	Our work reveals what happens when a comparable signal (AUROC 0.82) enters the GRPO training loop: the discriminability--utility gap is specific to gradient-based optimization, not to probe quality per se (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
18	
19	\paragraph{Process Rewards and Per-Step Supervision.}
20	SMART \citep{beigi2025smart} applies per-step process rewards with MCTS for sycophancy mitigation, representing an intermediate supervision density between our scalar per-completion GRPO rewards and dense per-token approaches like ACT.
21	The success of per-step rewards further supports our supervision density hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:discussion}): finer-grained reward signals may preserve enough behavioral structure to avoid the failure modes we document.
22	Preference-based methods such as DPO \citep{rafailov2023dpo} and KTO \citep{ethayarajh2024kto} operate on response pairs rather than scalar rewards and may naturally accommodate $\dsyc$-derived preference rankings; whether pair-wise optimization avoids the failures we observe in scalar GRPO remains an open question.
23	
24	\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}
25	Latent-GRPO \citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} extends GRPO with latent-space rewards for reasoning, providing richer gradient signal than scalar completion-level scores; \citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.
26	CRAFT \citep{luo2026craft} uses representation-level constraints during RL training to preserve alignment, operating at a denser granularity than our scalar per-completion approach.
27	These methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).
28	
```

</details>

whether pair-wise optimization avoids the failures we observe in scalar GRPO remains an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Representation-Guided RL.}\nLatent-GRPO \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} extends GRPO with latent-space rewards for reasoning, providing richer gradient signal than scalar completion-level scores; \\citet{sinii2025smallvectors} and \\citet{ackermann2026gradreg} study representation dynamics under RL.\nCRAFT \\citep{luo2026craft} uses representation-level constraints during RL training to preserve alignment, operating at a denser granularity than our scalar per-completion approach.\nThese methods succeed because target and suppressed behaviors are geometrically distinct; sycophancy breaks this condition, with revision and capitulation overlapping in direction space (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\n",

```json
    "newString": "The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration rather than grid search (Appendix~\\ref{app:training}).\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Framework}\n\\label{sec:framework}\n\n\\subsection{Direction Extraction}\n\\label{ssec:extraction}\n\nWe extract a linear sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$ from the residual stream using contrastive activation pairs: a \\emph{valid correction} (warranted belief revision under strong evidence) and a \\emph{sycophantic capitulation} (unwarranted concession under social pressure).\nWe construct 181 contrastive pairs using GPT-4.1 in a two-stage pipeline: skeleton generation followed by 3-turn dialogue completion.\nStyle matching is enforced by automated validation (response length difference ${<}10\\%$, shared opening phrase ${\\geq}2$ words), with up to 3 retries per pair; 19 of 200 initial pairs were discarded for failing validation.\nEvidence strength is annotated at three levels: \\emph{weak} (75 pairs; personal memory or opinion), \\emph{medium} (54 pairs; brief source citation), and \\emph{strong} (52 pairs; authoritative source with detailed data).\nFrom the 181 paired examples (362 activations total), we compute the mean difference at target layer $\\ell$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:dsyc}\n\\dsyc = \\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{i=1}^{N}\\left(\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i - \\mathbf{h}^{-}_i\\right)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{h}^{+}_i$ and $\\mathbf{h}^{-}_i$ are residual-stream activations for the $i$-th valid and sycophantic response.\nThe target layer is selected by 5-fold cross-validated AUROC; on Qwen3-8B this yields layer 19 with AUROC $0.827$ (5-fold CV $\\sigma{=}0.028$).\n\n\\subsection{Reward Formulations}\n\\label{ssec:reward}\n\nGRPO \\citep{shao2024grpo} ranks sampled completions by reward and updates the policy toward higher-reward outputs.\nWe define $R = w_1 \\cdot \\rone + w_3 \\cdot \\rthree$, where $\\rone$ is a direction-based component and $\\rthree$ is a behavioral component (DeBERTa NLI classifier scoring consistency with the ground-truth answer).\nThree formulations translate $\\dsyc$ into $\\rone$:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{F1}] \\textbf{Cosine projection} with four variants: (a) tanh-gated dot product $\\tanh(\\mathbf{h} \\cdot \\dsyc / 20)$, (b) raw cosine $\\cos(\\mathbf{h},\\, \\dsyc)$, (c) raw cosine with online recalibration (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}), and (d) $5{\\times}$ scaled cosine. See \\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure} for details.\n\\item[\\textbf{F2}] \\textbf{Binary probe.} $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$, a frozen logistic probe with stopped gradients.\n\\item[\\textbf{F3}] \\textbf{Continuous probe.} $\\rone = 1 - P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h})$, the sigmoid output of the same logistic probe, providing a continuous reward signal without discretization.\n\\end{itemize}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine term, then increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed $\\rone$ to contribute only ${\\sim}8.3\\%$ of total reward variance.\nThe empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.\nAs an ablation, R3-only GRPO sets $w_1{=}0$, training with only the behavioral reward.\n\n\\subsection{Online Recalibration}\n\\label{ssec:recalib}\n\nLoRA fine-tuning shifts internal representations, so we re-extract $\\dsyc$ every 500 steps.\nThe cosine similarity between consecutive directions remains ${\\approx}\\,0.96$ at step 500, indicating slow drift relative to recalibration frequency.\n\n\\subsection{SFT Baseline: Activation Consistency Training}\n\\label{ssec:baselines}\n\nWe implement Activation Consistency Training \\citep[ACT;][]{consistency2025bctact}, which combines standard SFT loss with an L2 activation consistency loss at target layers, providing dense per-token supervision.\nUnlike GRPO's single scalar per completion, ACT operates at the token level using labeled valid/invalid pairs.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\\label{ssec:eval}\n\nThe \\emph{True-positive Flip rate} ($\\tof$) measures acceptance of valid corrections; the \\emph{No-Flip rate} ($\\nof$) measures resistance to invalid pressure.\nTheir harmonic mean captures the calibration trade-off:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:sycon}\n\\sycon = \\frac{2 \\cdot \\tof \\cdot \\nof}{\\tof + \\nof}\n\\end{equation}\nWe use the harmonic mean to penalize methods that achieve high resistance by refusing all evidence-warranted corrections (low $\\tof$, high $\\nof$) or by capitulating universally (high $\\tof$, low $\\nof$).\nFlip classification uses \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base} (MNLI mismatched accuracy 90.0\\%, SNLI test 92.4\\%; \\citealt{he2023debertav3}) for stance classification, following \\citet{consistency2025bctact}.\nAll experiments use Qwen3-8B with LoRA (rank$=$64, $\\alpha{=}128$) and DeepSpeed ZeRO-2.\nWe test six $\\dsyc$-based GRPO configurations: four cosine $\\rone$ variants (F1a--F1d), binary probe $\\rone$ (F2), and continuous probe $\\rone$ (F3), plus $\\rthree$-only GRPO as a non-directional baseline and ActAdd as an inference-time baseline.\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Training Details}\n\\label{app:training}\n\nAll GRPO experiments use \\texttt{max\\_completion\\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\\% truncation of multi-turn responses.\nThis setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.\n\n\\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}\n\\label{app:taxonomy}\n\nManual inspection of 100 probe-trained responses (exp-008v3, step 2000) reveals three dominant failure modes.\n\\emph{Sycophantic stubbornness} (24\\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.\n\\emph{Deliberation traps} (29\\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.\n\\emph{Evidence-graded hedging}: under weak evidence, responses show surface compliance without substantive change; under strong evidence, responses add explicit hedging (``this is debatable,'' ``there are multiple perspectives'').\nThe model learns to modulate its \\emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \\emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.\n",
```

_source line 2853 · 2026-05-22T10:52:05.172Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01S3iHVfJNC2tsrJCyj5rB8j`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	Our RL training experiments use Qwen3-8B exclusively. We confirm cross-model discriminability of \dsyc{} on Llama-3-8B (AUROC $0.779 \pm 0.019$), but compute constraints (${\sim}200$ H100-equivalent hours) prevented replication of the reward formulation failures on a second model. Because the structural argument (that \dsyc{} cannot encode evidential warrant) is model-independent and the cross-model discriminability is the tested precondition for reward conversion, this limitation does not undermine the core claim.
4	
5	Our evaluation framework categorizes user challenges as binary valid or invalid. Real-world belief revision spans a continuous spectrum of evidential strength. The binary setup may overstate the difficulty of reward design if intermediate cases prove easier to separate in activation space.
6	
7	The style-controlled contrastive pairs isolate the cognitive signal from surface confounds (tone, length, formality) but may not capture the full diversity of sycophantic behavior in deployment. Natural sycophancy can involve subtler linguistic patterns absent from our constructed pairs.
8	
9	\sycon{} relies on a DeBERTa-based NLI classifier for flip determination rather than human annotation. Human judges might disagree on edge cases, though the NLI approach follows established evaluation protocols in the sycophancy literature. This affects measurement precision but not the direction of our negative results, since all reward formulations fail by large margins.
10	
11	Finally, our investigation is limited to GRPO. Other RL algorithms (PPO, DPO) may interact differently with representation-based rewards. However, the root cause of failure is that \dsyc{} is blind to evidential warrant, a property that is algorithm-independent.
```

</details>

```json
          " Our evaluation framework categorizes user challenges as binary valid or invalid. Real-world belief revision spans a continuous spectrum of evidential strength. The binary setup may overstate the difficulty of reward design if intermediate cases prove easier to separate in activation space.",
          " "
        ]
      }
```
